Adam Converges Without Any Modification On Update Rules
Yushun Zhang, Bingran Li, Congliang Chen, Zhi-Quan Luo, Ruoyu Sun

Abstract
Adam is the default algorithm for training neural networks, including large language models (LLMs). However, 71 provided an example that Adam diverges, raising concerns for its deployment in AI model training. We identify a key mismatch between the divergence example and practice: 71 pick the problem after picking the hyperparameters of Adam, i.e., ( β 1 , β 2 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2}) ; while practical applications often fix the problem first and then tune ( β 1 , β 2 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2}) . In this work, we prove that Adam converges with proper problem-dependent hyperparameters. First, we prove that Adam converges when β 2 \beta_{2} is large and β 1 < β 2 \beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}} . Second, when β 2 \beta_{2} is small, we point out a region of ( β 1 , β 2 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2}) combinations where Adam can diverge to infinity. Our results indicate a phase transition for Adam from divergence to convergence when changing the ( β 1 , β 2 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2}) combination. To our knowledge, this is the first phase transition in ( β 1 , β 2 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2}) 2D-plane reported in the literature, providing rigorous theoretical guarantees for Adam optimizer. We further point out that the critical boundary ( β 1 ∗ , β 2 ∗ ) (\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*}) is problem-dependent, and particularly, dependent on batch size. This provides suggestions on how to tune β 1 \beta_{1} and β 2 \beta_{2} : when Adam does not work well, we suggest tuning up β 2 \beta_{2} inversely with batch size to surpass the threshold β 2 ∗ \beta_{2}^{*} , and then trying β 1 < β 2 \beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}} . Our suggestions are supported by reports from several empirical studies, which observe improved LLM training performance when applying them.



†
†

\dagger
: Correspondence author.
†
†

This manuscript is an extended journal version of the conference paper “Adam Can Converge Without Any Modification On Update Rules” appeared at NeurIPS 2022. This manuscript contains a simplified proof for Adam under random shuffling (simplified based on the conference paper), and a new proof for Adam under with-replacement sampling (inspired by the conference paper).

1
Introduction

Machine learning tasks often aim to solve the following empirical risk minimization (ERM) problem.

\operatorname*{minimize}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\quad f(x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f_{i}(x),

(1.1)

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the trainable parameters,
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of mini-batches that partition the dataset, and
f_{i}(x)
denotes the loss on the
i
-th mini-batch data. For a fixed dataset of size
\mathcal{D}
, the batch size in each mini-batch is
\mathcal{D}/n
.
In deep learning, Adam
(
48
)
is one of the most popular algorithms for solving (
1.1
).
It has been applied to various domains such as natural language processing (NLP) and computer vision (CV) (e.g.,
(
80
;
28
)
).
Its impact is also evidenced by over 230,000 citations as of December 2025, a number that continues to grow rapidly
(
74
)
.

In the era of large language models (e.g.,
(
65
)
), Adam plays a central role in large-scale training.
Adam is reported to be used to train many mainstream LLMs, including Llama series
(
79
)
, Qwen series
(
4
)
, and DeepSeek series
(
56
)
, etc. Adam is clearly serving as a major horsepower behind the advancement of AI. Its influence was recently recognized when it received the
ICLR 2025 Test-of-Time Award

(
42
)
.

Despite its prevalence, an influential paper

(
71
)
(the winner of
ICLR 2018 Best Paper Award

(
41
)
) provides an example that
Adam diverges with a wide range of hyperparameters. A main result in
(
71
)
states that:

(
71
)
: For any
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
s.t.
0\leq\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}<1
, there exists a problem such that Adam diverges.

Here,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are the hyperparameters to control Adam’s 1st-order and 2nd-order momentum
m_{k}
and
v_{k}
.
The divergence region is visualized in Figure
1
(a). This finding raises serious concerns for Adam’s deployment in AI model training, where the divergence can raise alerts of unpredictable training failures. Since then, many new variants have been designed. For instance,
AMSGrad
(
71
)
enforces
v_{k}
(defined later in Algorithm
1
) to be non-decreasing; AdaBound
(
60
)
imposes constraint
v_{k}\in[C_{l},C_{u}]
to ensure the boundedness on the effective stepsize.

(a)

Divergent region claimed by
(
71
)

(b)

Our contribution

(c)

MNIST

(d)

CIFAR-10

Figure 1:

(a)
: The divergent region of Adam claimed by
(
71
)
. They fix
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
first and then pick a problem to construct the divergence example.
(b)
: An illustration of our contribution in
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
phase diagram. We fix the problem before picking
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. Note that this is a different setting from
(a)
, so there is no contradiction.
Both boundaries of the red and blue regions depend on batch size (shown later).
The shape of the region follows the solution to our analytic conditions. The dotted curve satisfies
\beta_{1}=\sqrt{\beta_{2}}
.
(c), (d)
: The training loss on MNIST and CIFAR-10. We sweep
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
in grids
\{(k_{1}/50,k_{2}/50)|k_{1}=0,\cdots,49,k_{2}=0,\cdots,49\}
, resulting in 2,500 trials. The performance of Adam reconciles with our theoretical characterization in
(b)
.

On the other hand, counter-intuitively, vanilla Adam remains exceptionally popular. Without any modification on its update rules, Adam works well in practice. This is rather surprising due to the existence of divergence theory.
Even more mysteriously, we find that the commonly reported hyperparameters actually satisfy the divergence condition stated earlier.
For instance,

48
claim that
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.9,0.999)
is a “good choice for the tested machine learning problems” and it is indeed the default setting in deep learning libraries.
GAN researchers (e.g.
70
;
44
) use
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.5,0.999)
. For LLMs such as GPT-3, Llama series, and DeepSeek series
(
11
;
79
;
56
)
,
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
is chosen to be
(0.9,0.95)
.
All these hyperparameters live in the divergence region
\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}
.
Surprisingly, instead of observing the divergence issue, these hyperparameters achieve good performances and they actually show the sign of convergence.

Why does Adam work well despite its theoretical divergence issue? Is there any mismatch between deep learning problems and the divergence example?
We take a closer look into the divergence example and find out the mismatch
does
exist.
In particular, we notice an important (but often ignored) characteristic of the divergence example:

(
71
)
picks
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

before
picking the problem (or precisely, the # mini-batches
n
).
That is, to construct the divergence example, they change
n
for different
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.
For instance, for
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0,0.99)
, they use one
n
to construct the divergent example; for
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0,0.9999)
, they use another
n
to construct another divergent example.
On the other hand, in practical applications of Adam listed above,
practitioners tune hyperparameters
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

after
the problem (or
n
) is fixed.
So there is a gap between the setting of theory and practice: the order of picking the problem and
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
is different.

Given the good practical performance of Adam, we conjecture that Adam can converge when the problem is fixed.
Unfortunately, the behavior of vanilla Adam is far less studied than its variants (perhaps due to the criticism of divergence).
To verify this conjecture, we run Adam for the classification problem on data MNIST and CIFAR-10 as shown in Figure
1
(c) and (d). For these two problems, we find that:
First, when
\beta_{2}
is large, the optimization error is small for almost all values of
\beta_{1}
; Second, when
\beta_{1},\ \beta_{2}
are both small, there is a
red region with 1.4x larger error.

While Adam’s performances seem unstable in the red region, we find that
it always performs well in the top blue region in Figure
1
.
This seems to suggest that: when the problem is fixed, Adam can converge without modification after proper tuning of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
.
We ask the following question:

Can Adam provably converge without any modification on its update rules?

In this work, we theoretically characterize Adam’s behavior across different choices of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.
We show that vanilla Adam (without algorithmic modification) exhibits two qualitatively different regimes:
in a
safe
region of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
it provably converges to the set of critical points (in the realizable case) or to a neighborhood of that set (in the non-realizable case),
whereas in a
danger
region it can diverge to infinity.
Together, these results reveal a divergence–convergence phase transition in the
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
plane.

Our contributions are visualized in Figure
1
(b) and summarized as follows.

•

Convergence for large
\beta_{2}
.

When
0\leq\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}<1
and
\beta_{2}
is larger than a problem-dependent threshold, Adam converges to critical points in the realizable case and to a neighborhood of critical points in the non-realizable case.
The threshold for
\beta_{2}
depends on the problem class, and it increases with
n
(equivalently, it decreases with batch size).

•

Divergence for small
\beta_{2}
.

For any
n\geq 3
and any problem-class parameters
(L,D_{0},D_{1})
with large enough
D_{1}
—where
L
is the smoothness constant (Assumption
2.1
) and
(D_{0},D_{1})
quantify the gradient variance condition (Assumption
2.2
)—there exists an instance in the problem class such that Adam diverges to infinity for all
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
in a certain region. The boundary of the divergence region expands with
n
(or decreases with batch size).

•

Key characteristics.

We emphasize the following aspects of our results.

(1) Phase transition
.
The divergence result considers the same setting as our convergence result (fixed problem class),
indicating the existence of a critical boundary
(\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*})
that demarcates a phase transition from divergence to convergence.

(2) Problem-dependent bounds
.
Our convergence and divergence regions of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
depend on the problem-class parameters. This is drastically different from
(
71
)
, which considers problem-independent
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

(3) Non-asymptotic characterization
.
Our “divergence region” of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
expands as
n
increases and converges to the whole region
[0,1)^{2}
as
n
goes to infinity,
which recovers (actually stronger than) the problem-independent divergence result of
(
71
)
.
In this sense, we can view the divergence result of
(
71
)

as an asymptotic characterization of the divergence region (as
n\rightarrow\infty
)
and our divergence result as a non-asymptotic characterization (for any fixed and finite
n
).

(4) No assumption of bounded gradients.

Our convergence analysis does
not
need a
bounded gradient assumption, which is commonly used in the literature.
Removing such assumption is crucial for revealing the phase transition: with gradients bounded a priori, the gradients of Adam cannot diverge, while we prove that it can happen under certain
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

•

The primary challenge in the convergence proof is characterizing the limit behavior of a stochastic non-linear dynamics.
We handle this by considering a concentration effect of Adam’s
1/\sqrt{v_{k}}
around
1/\sqrt{\mathbb{E}(v_{k})}
. One specific difficulty is that
v_{k}
can be arbitrarily close to 0, and
1/\sqrt{v_{k}}
can behave badly in the worst case. We find that a large
\beta_{2}
helps the concentration and stabilizes the dynamic system. The intuition is that a large
\beta_{2}
slows down the changes of
v_{k}
, and its behavior becomes predictable.

•

Tuning suggestions for
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

Our positive and negative results provide guidance for tuning
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
: when Adam does not work well, we suggest tuning up
\beta_{2}

inversely with batch size
to surpass the threshold
\beta_{2}^{*}
, and then trying
\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}
. These suggestions are supported by empirical findings in LLM pre-training
(
69
;
90
)
.

We believe our results advance the theoretical understanding of Adam.
While
71
state that “Adam can diverge”, our results show the other side of the story: when the problem is fixed, proper tuning can make Adam converge without any modification.
In particular, the convergence region of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
is guaranteed to be nonempty.
This is analogous to gradient descent on an
L
-smooth objective function, where a convergent stepsize always exists (e.g.
\eta<2/L
).
Overall, our results provide theoretical support for vanilla Adam and offer concrete guidance for selecting
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
in practice.

Relation to the conference version
(
95
)
.

This manuscript is an extended journal version of our NeurIPS 2022 conference paper
(
95
)
.
The current manuscript presents a streamlined convergence proof for Adam under random shuffling (greatly simplified relative to the conference version) and establishes a new convergence result and proof under with-replacement sampling, which was not covered in the conference version.
Technically, this journal version develops new concentration results along Adam’s trajectory and introduces a systematic procedure to deploy these concentration tools in the presence of momentum and unbounded gradients.
These changes help reduce the length of the random-shuffling proof from approximately
44
pages to about
18
pages, and they enable the with-replacement convergence analysis.
These proof techniques are new relative to the conference version and may be broadly applicable to analyzing Adam-type algorithms.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Review of Adam

We consider problem (
1.1
).
We use
x
to denote the optimization variable.
We denote
\nabla f_{i}
as the gradient of
f_{i}
.
We consider two implementations of Adam based on data sampling strategies: Adam under with-replacement sampling (Algorithm
1
) and under random shuffling (Algorithm
2
). Algorithm
1
is more theoretically oriented, and Algorithm
2
is more widely used in practical deployment of Adam
†
†

For instance, GPT-3 technical report states that ”Data are sampled without replacement during
training”
(
11
)
.
.

Algorithm 1

Adam under With-Replacement Sampling

Initialize
m_{0}
,
v_{0}
, and
x_{1}
.

for

k=1\to\infty

do

Sample
\tau_{k}
uniformly from
\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
.

m_{k}=\beta_{1}m_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{1})\nabla f_{\tau_{k}}(x_{k})

v_{k}=\beta_{2}v_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{2})\nabla f_{\tau_{k}}(x_{k})\circ\nabla f_{\tau_{k}}(x_{k})

x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\frac{\eta_{k}}{\sqrt{v_{k}}+\epsilon}\circ m_{k}

end

for

Algorithm 2

Adam under Random Shuffling

Initialize
m_{1,-1}
,
v_{1,-1}
, and
x_{1,0}
.

for

k=1\to\infty

do

Sample
\{\tau_{k,0},\tau_{k,1},\cdots,\tau_{k,n-1}\}
as a random permutation of
\{0,1,2,\cdots,n-1\}
.

for

i=0\to n-1

do

m_{k,i}=\beta_{1}m_{k,i-1}+\left(1-\beta_{1}\right)\nabla f_{\tau_{k,i}}(x_{k,i})

v_{k,i}=\beta_{2}v_{k,i-1}+\left(1-\beta_{2}\right)\nabla f_{\tau_{k,i}}(x_{k,i})\circ\nabla f_{\tau_{k,i}}(x_{k,i})

x_{k,i+1}=x_{k,i}-\frac{\eta_{k}}{\sqrt{v_{k,i}}+\epsilon}\circ m_{k,i}

end

for

x_{k+1,0}=x_{k,n}
;
v_{k+1,-1}=v_{k,n-1}
;
m_{k+1,-1}=m_{k,n-1}

end

for

In Algorithm
1
and
2
,
m
and
v
denote the 1st-order and 2nd-order momentum, respectively. The product
\circ
, division, and square-root operator are component-wise.
Regarding Algorithm
1
,
we denote
x_{k},m_{k},v_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the value of
x,m,v
at the
k
-th iteration, respectively.
Regarding Algorithm
2
,
we denote
x_{k,i},m_{k,i},v_{k,i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the value of
x,m,v
at the
k
-th outer loop (epoch) and the
i
-th inner loop (batch), respectively. We choose
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta_{0}}{\sqrt{k}}
as the stepsize.

In Algorithm
1
and
2
,
\epsilon
is adopted to avoid the corner case where
v_{k}
’s are constantly 0 along the trajectory, in which case Adam is not well-defined. To ensure the well-definedness of Adam, one can either use (i) a small
\epsilon
(e.g., default
\epsilon
is
10^{-8}
); or (ii) use
\epsilon=0
with a non-zero initialization
v\succ 0
. Our theory supports both cases. In the main body of the proof, we adopt (ii), which makes the results cleaner. We also provide the proof of case (i), i.e., the convergence of Adam with “non-zero
\epsilon
” in Appendix
E
. In either case, our final convergence results do not have any dependencies on
\epsilon
or initialization
v
, and thus our result remains non-vacuous regardless of
\epsilon
or
v
.

In the original paper by
(
48
)
, the authors introduce an additional “bias correction” step, which can be implemented by changing the stepsize
\eta_{k}
to
\hat{\eta}_{k}=\frac{\sqrt{1-\beta_{2}^{k}}}{1-\beta_{1}^{k}}\eta_{k}
. Note that
\hat{\eta}_{k}\in[\sqrt{1-\beta_{2}}\eta_{k},\frac{1}{1-\beta_{1}}\eta_{k}]
is well-bounded near
\eta_{k}
, so
\eta_{k}
and
\hat{\eta}_{k}
bring the same convergence rate. In the main body of our proof, we follow the forms of Algorithm
1
and
2
, which make the results cleaner. For completeness, we add the proof on the convergence of Adam with “bias correction” steps in Appendix
E
.

In our analysis, we make the following mild assumptions on
f_{i}(x)
and
f(x)
in the ERM problem (
1.1
).

Assumption 2.1
.

For any
i\in[n]
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\|_{2}\leq L\|x-y\|_{2}
.
In addition,
f(x)
is lower bounded by a finite constant
f^{*}
.

Assumption 2.2
.

\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq D_{1}\|\nabla f(x)\|_{2}^{2}+D_{0},\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
where
D_{1},D_{0}\geq 0
and are not both zero.

When
n,L,D_{0},
and
D_{1}
are fixed a priori, we define the corresponding problem class

\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:=\;\Bigr\{f(x)\Big|\ \ f(x)=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f_{i}(x),\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{and Assumptions~\ref{assum1}--\ref{assum2} hold with }(L,D_{0},D_{1})\Bigr\}.

(2.1)

In Assumption
2.1
, the Lipschitz condition for component functions is standard for ERM problems (e.g.,
(
9
;
7
;
12
;
72
;
3
)
).

Assumption
2.2
covers a class of variance or growth conditions in the literature.
We now discuss how Assumption
2.2
is reduced to different conditions under different choices of
D_{1}
and
D_{0}
.

We firstly discuss
D_{1}
. Under a slightly more restricted condition of
D_{1}>n
, Assumption
2.2
becomes the “affine variance” condition in (
2.2
), which controls the deviation between mini-batch and full gradient. The “affine variance” condition is originally proposed by
(
7
)
and is later popularized by
(
10
)
.

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)-\|\nabla f(x)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{(D_{1}-n)}{n}\|\nabla f(x)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{D_{0}}{n},\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.2)

When
D_{1}=n
, Assumption
2.2
and (
2.2
) reduce to the “bounded variance” in (
2.3
):

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)-\|\nabla f(x)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{D_{0}}{n},\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.3)

The “bounded variance” condition is widely used for analyzing both SGD and adaptive gradient methods (e.g.
(
68
;
34
;
33
;
89
)
). Assumption
2.2
allows the variance to grow with the gradient norm, making it strictly weaker than the bounded variance condition (
2.3
). This relaxation is meaningful: bounded variance is often too restrictive and can easily fail. For example, consider a convex quadratic minimization problem
(
10
)
:

\operatorname*{minimize}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\quad f(x)=\frac{1}{2n}\|Ax-b\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(a_{i}^{\top}x-b_{i})^{2}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f_{i}(x),

(2.4)

where
A
has rank at least 2. In this case, the left-hand side of (
2.3
) grows quadratically with
\|x\|_{2}
, and no finite
D_{0}
satisfies (
2.3
). In contrast, Assumption
2.2
holds with finite
D_{1}
and
D_{0}
. We provide a more detailed justification in Appendix
A.2
.

Another drawback of the bounded variance condition (
2.3
) is that it excludes some divergence counter-examples of SignSGD (which is equivalent to Adam with
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0
). As such, analysis under (
2.3
) may not reveal the full picture of Adam. We provide more detailed explanation later in Section
3.2
.

There is also a stronger condition that requires
D_{1}=0
, i.e.,
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq D_{0}
. This condition is sometimes called “bounded 2nd-order moment” (e.g.,
(
64
)
). This is strictly stronger than (
2.3
) and implies bounded gradient condition, so it is even more restricted.

As a result, Assumption
2.2
with generic
D_{1}
is recommended in
(
10
)
as it is
“relatively minor”
and
“variance is allowed to grow quadratically in any direction.”

Now we discuss
D_{0}
.
When
D_{0}=0
, the condition becomes “strong growth condition” (SGC)
(
76
;
73
)
.
An implication of SGC is that
when
\|\nabla f(x)\|=0
, we have
\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\right\|=0
for all
i
.
This condition is considered reasonable in the overparameterized regime where neural networks can interpolate all data points

(
81
;
75
)
.
We will show that Adam converges to exact critical points when
SGC holds.

For the general case where
D_{0}>0
, Adam is not guaranteed to reach the exact critical points.
Instead, it can only converge to a neighborhood of critical points
(
89
;
75
)
.
This phenomenon indeed occurs for Adam in experiments, even with diminishing stepsizes (see Figure
3
(b)).
This behavior is in line with a classical phenomenon in stochastic gradient methods: constant-stepsize SGD is known to converge to a neighborhood of critical points whose size scales with the noise level
(
61
;
8
;
87
;
88
;
58
)
.

Finally, we emphasize that we do
not
add the bounded gradient assumption
\|\nabla f(x)\|\leq G
, which is commonly used in the literature.
This is crucial for revealing the phase transition: with gradients bounded a priori, the gradients of Adam cannot diverge, while we prove that this can happen under certain
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

2.2
A Brief Review of the Counter-example in
(
71
)

Before stating our theoretical results, we first restate the counter-example by
(
71
)
. For the consistency of notation, we will restate their results under our notation.
They consider the following one-dimensional convex problem:
\operatorname*{minimize}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f_{i}(x)
where
x\in[-1,1]
,
n\geq 3
:

f_{i}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}nx,&\text{ for }i=0\\
-x,&\text{ otherwise, }\end{array}\right.

(2.5)

Note that (
2.5
) satisfies both Assumption
2.1
and
2.2
(with
D_{1}=n^{4}+n^{3}-n^{2}
and
D_{0}=0
), so our assumptions do not rule out this counter-example a priori.
Nevertheless, problem (
2.5
) is a constrained problem with feasible set
x\in[-1,1]
, and the optimal solution is
x^{*}=-1
.
Since this is a constrained problem, the term “divergence” here actually means the iterates will stay in a huge region with the size of the whole feasible set.
Since this is a constrained problem, the term “divergence” here actually means that the iterates will stay on the boundary of the feasible region and are far from the optimal solution
x*=-1
.
Note that both cases describe algorithmic behavior that is opposite to convergence, and we do not emphasize this distinction between them.

In
(
71
)
,
the function (
2.5
) is presented as an “online optimization problem” (not a finite-sum problem).
We rewrite (
2.5
) in a finite-sum form so that it matches our ERM notation in (
1.1
).
We will use the same cyclic sampling order

(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2};f_{0},f_{1},f_{2};\dots)
as in
71
.
For completeness,
we restate their main claim below in our notation.

Theorem 2.3

(Theorem 2 in
(
71
)
)
.

For any fixed
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
satisfying
\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}
, there exists a sufficiently large
n
, s.t., applying Adam to the function (
2.5
) (under cyclic sampling) converges to the sub-optimal point
x=1
.

We briefly discuss the divergent condition for this Theorem. As stated in Eq. (7), Appendix B in
(
71
)
, for every fixed
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
, they need an “
n
that depends on
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
”.
As such, they require different
n
to cause divergence on different
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. The considered problem class is constantly changing.

For completeness, we further restate Theorem 1 in
(
71
)
.

Theorem 2.4

(Theorem 1 in
(
71
)
)
.

For function (
2.5
), when
\beta_{1}=0
and
\beta_{2}=1/(n^{2}+1)
, Adam will converge to a sub-optimal point
x=1
.

This theorem considers choosing
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
after
n
. However, this result only shows divergence on
a single hyper-parameter choice

(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0,1/(n^{2}+1))
. This configuration lies somewhere on the left boundary of Figure
1
(a).
It therefore provides no characterization beyond this single point: it remains unclear which directions of changing
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
preserve divergence and which directions suppress it or induce convergence.

(a)

Cyclic update order

(b)

Randomized update order

(c)

Diminishing stepsize

Figure 2:
(a, b):
We restate Figure 1 from
(
71
)
. The divergence of Adam happens under both cyclic and update orders, so randomization cannot prevent divergence. Since they consider constrained problems, the term “divergence” here means getting stuck at the sub-optimal solution
x=1
. In the figures, AMSGrad is a different method, and it is not our focus.
(c):
Diminishing stepsize
\eta_{k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}
does not prevent divergence.

Finally, these divergence results also hold when randomized update orders are used instead of cyclic orders, as proved in
(
71
, Theorem 3)
. Consequently, randomization does not prevent divergence.
Further, the proof of
(
71
)
uses
\eta_{k}\propto\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}
, so diminishing stepsize does not prevent divergence, either. These claims are supported by numerical evidence in Figure
2
.

71
wrote:
“These results have important consequences insofar as one has to use problem-dependent
\epsilon
,
\beta_{1}
, and
\beta_{2}
in order to avoid bad convergence behavior. In high-dimensional problems, this typically amounts to using…a different
\epsilon
,
\beta_{1}
, and
\beta_{2}
for each dimension. However, this defeats the purpose of adaptive methods since it requires tuning a large set of parameters.”

In the following, we show that in our setting such divergence can be avoided without changing the algorithm or introducing new hyperparameters.
The key is to choose
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
in a problem-dependent manner, with a particular dependence on the number of mini-batches
n
(equivalently, the batch size).
Importantly, this does
not
require per-coordinate tuning of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
—a direction mentioned in the discussion of
71
.

2.3
Related Works

Ever since
71
pointed out the divergence issue, there are many attempts on designing new variants of Adam.
Since we focus on understanding vanilla Adam
without modification
, we do not discuss these variants here. We relegate the introduction to more Adam variants later in Appendix
A.1
.
Compared with proposing new variants,
the convergence of vanilla Adam is far less studied than its variants (perhaps due to the criticism of divergence). We discuss as follows.

75
study randomly-shuffled RMSprop proposed in
(
37
)
(Algorithm
2
with
\beta_{1}=0
)
†
†

We notice that
75
also analyze randomly-shuffled Adam with
\beta_{1}
close enough to
0
. However,
94
show that they require
\beta_{1}<10^{-7}
. Thus, their result does not provide much extra information other than randomly-shuffled RMSProp.
. We believe it is important to study Adam rather than RMSProp: numerically, Adam often substantially outperforms RMSProp (e.g.
(
1
)
). Furthermore, all mainstream LLMs are trained using Adam, and RMSProp is reported to perform
\geq 20\%
worse than Adam
(
66
, Table 1)
.
Theoretically, literature on RMSProp cannot reveal how these hyperparameters jointly affect (or jeopardize) the convergence of Adam in the
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-2D plane.
We note that
75
conjecture the existence of a critical threshold
\beta_{2}^{*}
for RMSProp. We generalize this conjecture to
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
-2D plane for Adam and confirm the existence of a critical boundary
(\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*})
.
One additional difference is that
75
only study randomly-shuffled RMSprop, whereas we consider more general cases of both with-replacement and randomly-shuffled Adam.

21
analyze Adam with
0<\beta_{2}<1
and
\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}
. However, they require bounded gradient assumption
\|\nabla f(x)\|\leq G
, which prevents the potential divergence a priori. Further, their bound is proportional to
1/\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is the stability hyperparameter and is usually small (e.g., default
\epsilon
is
10^{-8}
). As a result, they require sufficiently large non-zero
\epsilon
to keep the result non-vacuous. These assumptions incur artificial boundedness property on Adam:
0<\epsilon\leq\sqrt{v}+\epsilon\leq G
, and thus Adam will never diverge and is essentially changed to another algorithm called AdaBound
(
59
)
. In contrast, we do not impose these boundedness conditions and reveal the di-convergence phase transition under different
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

New proofs of Adam after the online appearance of the conference version in 2022.

After the conference version of this manuscript
(
95
)
appeared online in August 2022, there has been a series of works providing new convergence proofs of Adam. Here, we summarize how their assumptions differ from ours.

Recent works
(
86
;
53
;
52
)
provide new proofs for RMSProp and Adam. Compared to our work, their proof relies on a “coordinate-wise bounded variance” condition, which imposes the inequality of (
2.3
) on each coordinate individually.
Note that this is stronger than (
2.3
), which only imposes the inequality on the overall gradient. Moreover, since (
2.3
) is already known to be restrictive— for instance, it fails even on simple quadratic problems (
2.4
)—its coordinate-wise variant is necessarily more limiting.
Besides the coordinate-wise bounded variance, they also required
T
-dependent
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
, where
T
is the pre-determined total iteration budget. In practice,
T
-dependent
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
is rarely used or reported by practitioners. In theory, we argue that such dependence on
T
is unnecessary and can be removed. In contrast, our proof does not require these two conditions.

82
provide a novel convergence proof for Adam under the “coordinate-wise affine variance” condition and
T
-dependent
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. Similarly, the coordinate-wise version is strictly stronger than the affine variance condition (
2.2
) or Assumption
2.2
; and the
T
-dependence is unnecessary.
83
further relax these two conditions, but their analysis focused on Adam under random shuffling and does not cover with-replacement sampling.

51
;
67
;
38
;
39
;
92
provide refined convergence proofs of Adam. One limitation is that their complexity is proportional to
1/\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is the stability hyperparameter. These bounds
become vacuous as
\epsilon
approaches
0
, which brings concerns since
\epsilon
is usually small in practice (e.g., default
\epsilon
is
10^{-8}
). We argue that the dependency on
\epsilon
is artificial, and in contrast, our result is independent of
\epsilon
and allows arbitrarily small
\epsilon
including 0. In addition to the dependency on
\epsilon
,
51
;
67
require the bounded variance condition (
2.3
) or its stronger version; and
38
;
39
;
92
require an almost-sure or coordinate-wise version of the affine variance condition (
2.2
). All these assumptions are stronger than Assumption
2.2
.

Another line of new works prove the convergence of Adam under bounded gradient or iterate, or bounded
v_{k}
assumptions
(
27
;
47
;
84
;
55
)
, while we do not impose these boundedness conditions.

Besides the difference in assumptions, we further highlight an important difference between our work and the literature above. The above works focus on proving convergence upper bounds for Adam under certain choices of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. However, a good upper bound is just one side of the story. In contrast, we establish the
phase transition from divergence to convergence
in different regions of
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
, which presents a more complete picture. Our work points out two missing facts in the literature.

•

First
, we show that tuning
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
is not only sufficient
but also necessary
for convergence. Specifically, we prove the existence of a phase transition: as
\beta_{2}
increases from 0 to 1, the behavior of Adam shifts from divergence (a bad lower bound in Theorem
3.5
) to convergence (good upper bounds in Theorem
3.1
and
3.3
). This reveals that tuning
\beta_{2}
is not merely about optimizing the convergence rate; it is crucial for preventing the fundamental divergence danger. To our knowledge, this is the first phase transition in
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
2D-plane for Adam reported in the literature.

•

Second
, we show that this phase transition occurs at a
problem-dependent
boundary
(\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*})
, which grows
inversely with batch size
.
This contrasts with prior theoretical works that suggest
\beta_{2}
should increase with the total iteration budget
T
and does not reveal the dependency on the considered problem.

3
Main Results

3.1
Convergence Results

Now we prove that Adam converges with proper problem-dependent hyperparameters.
We first present the result for Algorithm
1
and then present the result for Algorithm
2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

(Convergence result for Algorithm
1
)

Assume Algorithm
1
satisfies:

0\leq\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}<1
;
\beta_{2}
satisfies
\beta_{2}\geq\gamma_{1}(n):=1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1-\beta_{1}^{n}}{n^{5}}\right)
; and
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta_{0}}{\sqrt{k}}
. For any
f(x)\in\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, when
T\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[1,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\frac{\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{D_{0}}},\frac{\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|_{2}}{2\sqrt{dD_{1}}}\right\}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log T}{\sqrt{T}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\delta(\beta_{2})\sqrt{D_{0}}),

where
\delta(\beta_{2})
is a positive constant that approaches 0 as
\beta_{2}
approaches 1 (see (
6.46
) in Section
6
).

(a)

batch size v.s.
\beta_{2}

(b)

Large
\beta_{2}
and
D_{0}>0

(c)

Large
\beta_{2}
and
D_{0}=0

Figure 3:
(a):
The training
loss of Adam on MNIST under different batch size and
\beta_{2}
. The trend aligns with our theory: we need a larger
\beta_{2}
when batch size is small. Here, we used the default
\beta_{1}=0.9
.

(b) (c):

large-
\beta_{2}
Adam converges to a neighborhood of critical points when
D_{0}>0
and converges to exact critical points when
D_{0}=0
. We use diminishing stepsize
\eta_{k}=0.1/\sqrt{k}
as in our theory. Experimental details are shown in Appendix
F
.

Remark 1: the choice of
\beta_{2}
.

Our theory suggests that large
\beta_{2}
should be used to ensure convergence. This message matches our experiments in Figure
1
. We emphasize that the requirement of “large
\beta_{2}
” is necessary, because small
\beta_{2}
will indeed lead to provable divergence (shown later in Theorem
3.5
).
We here comment a bit on the threshold
\gamma_{1}(n)
. By the proof of Theorem
3.1
, and particularly, by (
6.43
) in Section
6
,
\beta_{2}
needs to satisfy
\beta_{2}\geq 1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1-\beta_{1}^{n}}{n^{5}}\right)
. We also remark that
\gamma_{1}(n)
slowly increases with
\beta_{1}
. This property is visualized in Figure
1
(b) where the lower boundary of the blue region slightly lifts up when
\beta_{1}
increases.
Note that our threshold of
\beta_{2}
is a sufficient condition for convergence and the power is not claimed tight. Tightening the threshold for
\beta_{2}
will be an interesting future direction.

Remark 2:
\beta_{2}
and batch size.

We find that the condition of
\beta_{2}
:
\beta_{2}\geq\gamma_{1}(n)=1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1-\beta_{1}^{n}}{n^{5}}\right)
is problem-dependent and it increases with
n
. This property suggests that we need larger
\beta_{2}
when
n
is large, or equivalently, we need larger
\beta_{2}
when the batch size is small
†
†

This is because batch size equals
\frac{\mathcal{D}}{n}
, where
\mathcal{D}
denotes the total sample size and
n
denotes the number of mini-batches into which the dataset is divided.
. This aligns with our experiments in Fig.
3
(a): on MNIST, smaller batch size
\frac{\mathcal{D}}{n}
(which is equivalent to larger
n
) requires a larger
\beta_{2}
to reach small loss.

Remark 3: the choice of
\beta_{1}
.

Theorem
3.1
requires
\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}
.
Since
\beta_{2}
is suggested to be large, our convergence result can cover flexible choice of
\beta_{1}\in[0,1)
. For instance,
\beta_{2}=0.999
brings the threshold of
\beta_{1}<0.9995
, which covers basically all practical choices of
\beta_{1}
reported in the literature, including the default setting
\beta_{1}=0.9
. Our theory aligns with our grid-search experiments on MNIST and CIFAR-10 (Figure
1
(c,d) in Section
1
), where Adam shows good performance for a wide range of
\beta_{1}
when
\beta_{2}
is large.

Remark 4: guidance to LLM pre-training.

Our theory indicates that larger
\beta_{2}
is required when
n
is large. This equivalently indicates that a larger
\beta_{2}
is required when the batch size, which equals
\frac{\mathcal{D}}{n}
, is small. Our subsequent divergence theory (presented later) further implies that making
\beta_{2}
dependent on the batch size is necessary to avoid divergence. Collectively, these messages can provide guidance for hyperparameter tuning in LLM pre-training, as confirmed by various literature. We list some numerical evidence as follows.

•

96
;
66
report that training fails if both
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
are close to 0, and the performance substantially improves when use
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0.95
or 0.975. This finding aligns with our results. We restate some of their numerical results in Figure
4
(a).

•

77
state that
“
95
shows that higher
\beta_{2}
values substantially improve small-batch training, and
63
highlights the importance of scaling
\beta_{2}
in this regime.”
We restate some of their numerical results in Figure
4
(b) and (c).

•

90
emphasize that
“larger
\beta_{2}
(increase from 0.95 to 0.99, 0.999, or 0.9995) substantially improves small batch size training … aligning with findings in
(
95
)
”
. We restate some of their numerical results in Figure
4
(d, e).

•

69
report that
“enlarging
\beta_{2}
(from 0.95 to 0.99 and 0.999) is essential at lower batch sizes … This matches the theoretical work
(
95
)
.”

Remark 5: convergence to a neighborhood of critical points.

When
D_{0}>0
, Adam converges to a neighborhood of critical points, in lieu of the exact critical points. We emphasize that this is
not
due to the limitation of the analysis, and this phenomenon is also observed in practice: even for simple convex quadratic function with
D_{0}>0
, Adam with diminishing stepsize
cannot
reach exactly zero gradient (see Figure
3
(b). We state the non-realizable function in Appendix
F
).

In the non-realizable case (
D_{0}>0
), converging to the neighborhood of critical points is common for stochastic gradient methods, including constant-stepsize SGD
(
61
;
8
;
87
;
88
;
58
)
and diminishing-stepsize RMSProp
(
89
;
75
)
. This is because: even though
\eta_{k}
is decreasing, the effective stepsize
\eta_{k}/\sqrt{v_{k}}
might not decay.
The good news is that the size of the neighborhood
\mathcal{O}(\delta(\beta_{2})\sqrt{D_{0}})
vanishes to 0 as
\beta_{2}
goes to 1 (both in theory and experiments in Figure
3
(b)). This can be seen in the expression of
\delta(\beta_{2})
in (
6.46
) in Appendix
6
. The size shrinks to 0 because the movement of
\sqrt{v_{k}}
shrinks as
\beta_{2}
increases.

(a)

Fig. 12 from
(
66
)

(b)

Fig. 5 from
(
77
)

(c)

Fig.4 from
(
63
)

(d)

Figure 8 (b) from
(
90
)

(e)

Table 4 from
(
90
)

On the effect of
\beta_{2}
on LLM pre-training from recent literature.
(a,b,c)
: Final validation loss of LLMs trained under different
\beta_{2}
and batch size.
(c):
greener color indicates lower validation loss.
(d, e):
The optimal
\beta_{2}
to train a LLM with 1.2B parameters under different batch size. Here,
\tau
serves for other training tricks that are independent of our discussion. These results reach a consistent conclusion: Larger
\beta_{2}
helps boost performance and shall be tuned up under small batch-size regimes. These results confirm that our theory provides valid guidance for hyperparameter tuning in LLM pre-training.

As a corollary of Theorem
3.1
, we have the following result in the realizable case (i.e.,
D_{0}=0
).

Corollary 3.2
.

Under the setting in Theorem
3.1
. When
D_{0}=0
for Assumption
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\vskip-5.69054pt\min_{k\in[1,T]}\mathbb{E}\|\nabla f(x_{k})\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log T}{\sqrt{T}}\right).\vskip-5.69054pt

In the realizable case (i.e.
D_{0}=0
), Corollary
3.2
states that Adam can converge to critical points. This matches our numerical results in Figure
3
(c). The convergence rate in Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
is comparable to that of SGD under the same condition in
(
81
)
.

Similarly to Theorem
3.1
, we now present the convergence result for Adam under random shuffling (Algorithm
2
). All the remarks above also apply to the random-shuffling case.

Theorem 3.3
.

(Convergence result for Algorithm
2
)

Assume that Algorithm
2
satisfies:
\beta_{1}<\sqrt{\beta_{2}}<1
;
\beta_{2}
is greater than or equal to a threshold
\gamma_{2}(n):=1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1-\beta_{1}^{n}}{n^{5.5}}\right)
; and
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta_{0}}{\sqrt{k}}
.
For any
f(x)\in\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
when
T\in\mathbb{N}
is sufficiently large, we have:

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[1,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\frac{\|\nabla f(x_{k,0})\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{D_{0}}},\frac{\|\nabla f(x_{k,0})\|_{2}}{2\sqrt{dD_{1}}}\right\}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log T}{\sqrt{T}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\tilde{\delta}(\beta_{2})\sqrt{D_{0}}),

where
\tilde{\delta}(\beta_{2})
is a positive constant that approaches 0 as
\beta_{2}
approaches 1.

As a corollary of Theorem
3.3
, we have the following result in the realizable case (i.e.,
D_{0}=0
).

Corollary 3.4
.

Under the setting in Theorem
3.3
. When
D_{0}=0
for Assumption
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[1,T]}\mathbb{E}\|\nabla f(x_{k,0})\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log T}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.3
can be seen in Section
5
–
7
and Appendix
D
, respectively.

3.2
Divergence Results

Now we prove that small-
\beta_{2}
Adam can diverge, and the divergence region is problem-dependent.
The divergence of small-
\beta_{2}
Adam suggests that “large
\beta_{2}
” is necessary for Theorem
3.1
.

We construct a counter-example in
\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consider
f(x)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f_{i}(x)
for
n\geq 3
and
x\in\mathbb{R}

, we define
f_{i}(x)
as:

f_{0}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(1+(n-1)a\right)x,&x\geq-1\\
\frac{\left(1+(n-1)a\right)}{2}(x+2)^{2}-\frac{3n}{2},&x<-1\end{array}\right.,\quad f_{i}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-ax,&x\geq-1\\
-\frac{a}{2}(x+2)^{2}+\frac{3}{2},&x<-1\end{array}\right.\quad\text{for $i>0$,}\vskip-2.84526pt

(3.1)

where
a>0
. Summing up all the
f_{i}(x)
, one can see that: for any finite positive
a>0
,
f(x)
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For instance, when
a=1/(n-1)^{2}
, we have
D_{1}=2n^{2}
,
D_{0}=0
, and
f(x)

is lower bounded with optimal
x^{*}=-2
. Further, it satisfies Assumption
2.2
but not bounded variance (
2.3
), which restricts
D_{1}
to be
n
.
Function (
3.1
) is modified based on
(
71
)
but it allows both iterates and gradients to diverge to infinity.
We present the divergence result in Theorem
3.5
. We note that Sign-SGD is a special case of Adam with
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0
, so the following divergence result applies to Sign-SGD as well.

Theorem 3.5
.

For any problem class
\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
n\geq 3
,
D_{1}\geq 2n^{2}
, and
D_{0}\geq 0
, there exists an
f(x)\in\mathcal{F}_{L,D_{0},D_{1}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, s.t. when
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
satisfies analytic conditions (
8.3
), (
8.4
), (
8.5
) in Section
8
, Adam’s iterates, gradients of the iterates, and function values of the iterates all diverge to infinity.
The size of the region depends on
n
and it expands to the whole region
[0,1]^{2}
as
n
grows to infinity.

(a)

Divergence region

(b)

The effect of small
\beta_{2}

(a):
On function (
3.1
) with

and

), (

.
The blue curve satisfies

.

When

is small, Adam diverges. We use function (

) with initialization
x=-5
and
n=20
. The labels in (b) stand for
[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]
.

The proof can be seen in Section
8
. We find
the “divergence region” is problem-dependent. Moreover, it
always stays below the “convergence threshold”
\gamma_{1}(n)
in Theorem
3.1
, so the two results are self-consistent (see the remark in Section
8
). The divergence of small-
\beta_{2}
Adam is also observed numerically (see Figure
5
(b)).
These results characterize Adam’s divergence behavior both numerically and theoretically.

In Figure
6
, we provide
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
grid-search on the counter-example (
3.1
). We find that Adam’s behavior aligns with our prediction in theory: when initialized in the linear side of function (
3.1
), i.e.,
x\geq-1
, the iterates will keep moving rightwards when
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are both small, causing divergence. The divergence region expands with
n
. Similarly, when initialized in the quadratic region, i.e.,
x<-1
, the iterates will keep moving leftwards, so the gradient norm also diverges (this is also shown in Figure
5
(b)).

Finally, we emphasize that the orange region in Figure
